\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{\BGO(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda_{\mathcal{C}}}
\textstyle{\BPGO(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}^{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}^{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}

Proof.

Commutativity of the diagram is clear, and the lower row is exact by
definition of
\BPGO(\mathcal{C})
. Step 1 of the proof of
Proposition
2.18
shows that
\lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\BHomot(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}^{3}
is surjective, hence the left column is
exact. Moreover, the right column is exact by
Proposition
2.18
; therefore it only remains to
prove that the canonical map
\BOrth(\mathcal{C})\to\BPGO(\mathcal{C})
is
surjective. Since
\BPGO(\mathcal{C})
is smooth, it suffices to consider
the group of rational points over an algebraic closure
F_{\text{\rm alg}}
of

F
. We know
\lambda_{\mathcal{C}}\colon\BHomot(\mathcal{C})\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}^{3}
is surjective,
hence for every
g\in\BGO(\mathcal{C})(F_{\text{\rm alg}})
there exists

h\in\BHomot(\mathcal{C})(F_{\text{\rm alg}})
such that

\lambda_{\mathcal{C}}(g)=\lambda_{\mathcal{C}}(h)
. Then

gh^{-1}
lies in
\BOrth(\mathcal{C})(F_{\text{\rm alg}})
and has the same image in

\BPGO(\mathcal{C})(F_{\text{\rm alg}})
as
g
, hence the canonical map

\BOrth(\mathcal{C})(F_{\text{\rm alg}})\to\BPGO(\mathcal{C})(F_{\text{\rm alg}})
is surjective.
∎

Let
\homot(\mathcal{C})
and
\mathfrak{pgo}(\mathcal{C})
be the Lie algebras of

\BHomot(\mathcal{C})
and
\BPGO(\mathcal{C})
respectively. By definition,

\homot(\mathcal{C})=\{(\nu_{1}\operatorname{Id}_{V_{1}},\nu_{2}\operatorname{Id}_{V_{2}},\nu_{3}\operatorname{Id}_{V_{3}},\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-\nu_{3})\mid\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\nu_{3}\in F\}\simeq F\times F\times F.

On the other hand, since
\BHomot(\mathcal{C})
is smooth, the canonical map

\BGO(\mathcal{C})\to\BPGO(\mathcal{C})
is separable by
[
13
, (22.13)]
,
hence its differential is surjective. Therefore,

\mathfrak{pgo}(\mathcal{C})=\mathfrak{go}(\mathcal{C})/\homot(\mathcal{C}).

The following result yields an explicit description of
\mathfrak{pgo}(\mathcal{C})

for use in §
3.3
:

Proposition 2.20
.

Mapping
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},\lambda_{3})+\homot(\mathcal{C})\in\mathfrak{pgo}(\mathcal{C})
to

(g_{1}+F,\,g_{2}+F,\,g_{3}+F)\in\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{1})\times\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{2})\times\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{3})
identifies
\mathfrak{pgo}(\mathcal{C})
with the subgroup of

\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{1})\times\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{2})\times\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{3})
consisting of triples

(g_{1}+F,\,g_{2}+F,\,g_{3}+F)
where
g_{1}\in\mathfrak{go}(q_{1})
,
g_{2}\in\mathfrak{go}(q_{2})
and

g_{3}\in\mathfrak{go}(q_{3})
satisfy (
2.26
) for some

\lambda_{3}\in F
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\homot(\mathcal{C})
is the kernel of the map

\mathfrak{go}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{1})\times\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{2})\times\mathfrak{pgo}(q_{3})
carrying

(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},\lambda_{3})
to
(g_{1}+F,\,g_{2}+F,\,g_{3}+F)
. Clearly,

\homot(\mathcal{C})
lies in the kernel of this map. Conversely, if

(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},\lambda_{3})
lies in the kernel, then there are scalars

\nu_{1}
,
\nu_{2}
,
\nu_{3}\in F
such that
g_{i}=\nu_{i}\operatorname{Id}_{V_{i}}
for

i=1
,
2
,
3
. Then (
2.26
) yields

\lambda_{3}=\nu_{1}+\nu_{2}-\nu_{3}
, hence
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},\lambda_{3})
lies
in
\homot(\mathcal{C})
.
∎

Remark 2.21
.

If
\charac F\neq 2
, the upper row of the diagram in
Proposition
2.19
shows that the canonical map

\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathfrak{pgo}(\mathcal{C})
is an isomorphism, for then
\BZO(\mathcal{C})

is smooth and its Lie algebra is
0
. This canonical map is
not

bijective if
\charac F=2
, even though
\mathfrak{o}(\mathcal{C})
and

\mathfrak{pgo}(\mathcal{C})
have the same dimension.

2.4.
Compositions of pointed quadratic spaces

Fixing a representation of
1
in a quadratic space yields a new
structure:

Definitions 2.22
.

A
pointed quadratic space
over an arbitrary field
F
is a
triple
(V,q,e)
where
(V,q)
is a quadratic space with nonsingular
polar form over
F
and
e\in V
is a vector such that

q(e)=1
. Each pointed quadratic space is endowed with a canonical
isometry
\overline{\rule{7.11317pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}}
of order
2
, defined by

\overline{x}=e\,b(e,x)-x\qquad\text{for $x\in V$,}

where
b
is the polar form of
q
. Isometries of
pointed quadratic
spaces are required to preserve the distinguished vector
representing
1
.

A
composition of pointed quadratic spaces
over
F
is a

4
-tuple

(2.30)

\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}=\bigl((V_{1},q_{1},e_{1}),\,(V_{2},q_{2},e_{2}),\,(V_{3},q_{3},e_{3}),\,*_{3}\bigr)

where
(V_{1},q_{1},e_{1})
,
(V_{2},q_{2},e_{2})
,
(V_{3},q_{3},e_{3})
are pointed
quadratic spaces over
F
and the
4
-tuple

\mathcal{C}:=\bigl((V_{1},q_{1})\,(V_{2},q_{2}),\,(V_{3},q_{3}),\,*_{3}\bigr)
obtained
by forgetting the distinguished vectors is a
composition of quadratic spaces such that

e_{1}*_{3}e_{2}=e_{3}.

It follows from the definition that
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
and
q_{3}

represent
1
, hence these forms are isometric Pfister forms, by
Proposition
2.7
,
2.8
or

2.9
(depending on the dimension). Note that
(
2.10
) readily yields

e_{2}*_{1}e_{3}=e_{1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad e_{3}*_{2}e_{1}=e_{2}.

Therefore, the following are compositions
of pointed quadratic spaces:

\displaystyle\partial\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=\bigl((V_{2},q_{2},e_{2}),(V_{3},q_{3},e_{3}),(V_{1},q_{1},e_{1}),*_{1}),

\displaystyle\partial^{2}\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}

\displaystyle=\bigl((V_{3},q_{3},e_{3}),(V_{1},q_{1},e_{1}),(V_{2},q_{2},e_{2}),*_{2}).

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}
be compositions of
pointed quadratic spaces, and let
\mathcal{C}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
be the
compositions of quadratic spaces obtained from
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
and

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}
by forgetting the distinguished vectors. Every
similitude
f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
that preserves the
distinguished vectors must be an isometry, because the equations

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\,f_{1}(x_{2}*_{1}x_{3})

\displaystyle=f_{2}(x_{2})\,\widetilde{*}_{1}\,f_{3}(x_{3}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{2}\,f_{2}(x_{3}*_{2}x_{1})

\displaystyle=f_{3}(x_{3})\,\widetilde{*}_{2}\,f_{1}(x_{1}),

\displaystyle\lambda_{3}\,f_{3}(x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})

\displaystyle=f_{1}(x_{1})\,\widetilde{*}_{3}\,f_{2}(x_{2})

for
x_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
,
x_{3}\in V_{3}
imply

\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}=1
if
f(e_{i})=\widetilde{e}_{i}
for
i=1
,

2
,
3
. Therefore, between compositions of pointed quadratic spaces
the only type of maps we consider are isomorphisms.

Definition 2.23
.

An
isomorphism

f\colon\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}
of
compositions of pointed quadratic spaces is an isomorphism

f\colon\mathcal{C}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
of compositions of quadratic spaces
that maps the distinguished vectors of
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
to the
distinguished vectors of
\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}^{\bullet}
. The automorphisms of

\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
define an algebraic group scheme

\BOrth(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
, which is a closed subgroup of

\BOrth(\mathcal{C})
.

Our goal in this subsection is to show that every composition of pointed
quadratic spaces
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
carries a canonical isomorphism

\Delta\colon\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}\to\partial\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
and is isomorphic
to a composition
S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
such that
\partial S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})=S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
. For this, we will use the following
identities relating the canonical isometry
\overline{\rule{7.11317pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}}
and multiplication
by the distinguished vectors:

Lemma 2.24
.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
be a composition of pointed quadratic spaces as
in (
2.30
).

(a)

For every
x_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
,
x_{3}\in V_{3}
,

\displaystyle\overline{e_{1}*_{3}x_{2}}

\displaystyle=e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}},

\displaystyle\overline{x_{1}*_{3}e_{2}}

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{1}}*_{3}e_{2},

\displaystyle\overline{e_{2}*_{1}x_{3}}

\displaystyle=e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x_{3}},

\displaystyle\overline{x_{2}*_{1}e_{3}}

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{2}}*_{1}e_{3},

\displaystyle\overline{e_{3}*_{2}x_{1}}

\displaystyle=e_{3}*_{2}\overline{x_{1}},

\displaystyle\overline{x_{3}*_{2}e_{1}}

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{3}}*_{2}e_{1}.

(b)

For every
x_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
,
x_{3}\in V_{3}
,

\displaystyle e_{1}*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}x_{1})

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{1}}*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}e_{1}),

\displaystyle(x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})*_{2}e_{1}

\displaystyle=(e_{1}*_{3}x_{2})*_{2}\overline{x_{1}},

\displaystyle e_{2}*_{1}(x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{2}}*_{1}(x_{1}*_{3}e_{2}),

\displaystyle(x_{2}*_{1}x_{3})*_{3}e_{2}

\displaystyle=(e_{2}*_{1}x_{3})*_{3}\overline{x_{2}},

\displaystyle e_{3}*_{2}(x_{2}*_{1}x_{3})

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{3}}*_{2}(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3}),

\displaystyle(x_{3}*_{2}x_{1})*_{1}e_{3}

\displaystyle=(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})*_{1}\overline{x_{3}}.

(c)

For every
x_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
,
x_{3}\in V_{3}
,

\displaystyle e_{2}*_{1}\bigl(e_{1}*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{1}}=\bigl((x_{1}*_{3}e_{2})*_{2}e_{1}\bigr)*_{1}e_{3},

\displaystyle e_{3}*_{2}\bigl(e_{2}*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}x_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{2}}=\bigl((x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})*_{3}e_{2})*_{2}e_{1},

\displaystyle e_{1}*_{3}\bigl(e_{3}*_{2}(e_{2}*_{1}x_{3})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\overline{x_{3}}=\bigl((x_{3}*_{2}e_{1})*_{1}e_{3}\bigr)*_{3}e_{2}.

(d)

For every
x_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
,
x_{3}\in V_{3}
,

\displaystyle\overline{x_{1}*_{3}x_{2}}=(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})

\displaystyle=\bigl((e_{3}*_{2}\overline{x_{1}})*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}}\bigr)*_{3}e_{2},

\displaystyle\overline{x_{2}*_{1}x_{3}}=(x_{3}*_{2}e_{1})*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}x_{2})

\displaystyle=\bigl((e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}})*_{2}(e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x_{3}})\bigr)*_{1}e_{3},

\displaystyle\overline{x_{3}*_{2}x_{1}}=(x_{1}*_{3}e_{2})*_{2}(e_{2}*_{1}x_{3})

\displaystyle=\bigl((e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x_{3}})*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}\overline{x_{1}})\bigr)*_{2}e_{1}.

Proof.

To avoid repetitions, we just prove the first formulas in each case.

(a)
By definition,

\overline{e_{1}*_{3}x_{2}}=e_{3}\,b_{3}(e_{3},e_{1}*_{3}x_{2})-e_{1}*_{3}x_{2}.

Substituting
e_{1}*_{3}e_{2}
for
e_{3}
and using
b_{3}(e_{3},e_{1}*_{3}x_{2})=b_{2}(e_{3}*_{2}e_{1},x_{2})=b_{2}(e_{2},x_{2})
yields

\overline{e_{1}*_{3}x_{2}}=e_{1}*_{3}(e_{2}\,b_{2}(e_{2},x_{2})-x_{2})=e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}}.

(b)
By (
2.18
) and (
2.12
),

e_{1}*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}x_{1})=x_{3}\,b_{1}(e_{1},x_{1})-x_{1}*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}e_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad x_{3}=e_{1}*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}e_{1}),

hence

e_{1}*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}x_{1})=(e_{1}\,b_{1}(e_{1},x_{1})-x_{1})*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}e_{1})=\overline{x_{1}}*_{3}(x_{3}*_{2}e_{1}).

(c)
Using (b) and (
2.10
), we have

e_{2}*_{1}\bigl(e_{1}*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})\bigr)=e_{2}*_{1}\bigl(\overline{x_{1}}*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}e_{1})\bigr)=e_{2}*_{1}(\overline{x_{1}}*_{3}e_{2})=\overline{x_{1}}.

(d)
We compute
(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})
by using
(
2.18
) to exchange the factors
x_{2}*_{1}e_{3}
and

x_{1}
:

(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})=e_{3}\,b_{1}(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3},x_{1})-x_{1}*_{3}\bigl(e_{3}*_{2}(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})\bigr).

Since
e_{3}*_{2}(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})=x_{2}
by (
2.11
) and

b_{1}(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3},x_{1})=b_{3}(e_{3},x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})
by (
2.1
), it
follows that

(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})=e_{3}\,b_{3}(e_{3},x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})-x_{1}*_{3}x_{2}=\overline{x_{1}*_{3}x_{2}}.

On the other hand, (b) yields

x_{2}*_{1}e_{3}=x_{2}*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}e_{2})=e_{2}*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}}),

hence, using (b) again together with (a),

(x_{2}*_{1}e_{3})*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})=\bigl(e_{2}*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}})\bigr)*_{3}(e_{3}*_{2}x_{1})\\
=\bigl(\overline{e_{3}*_{2}x_{1}}*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}})\bigr)*_{3}e_{2}=\bigl((e_{3}*_{2}\overline{x_{1}})*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}})\bigr)*_{3}e_{2}.\qed

For a composition of pointed quadratic spaces
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
as
in (
2.30
), we define a composition of pointed
quadratic spaces
S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
as follows:

(2.31)

S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})=\bigl((V_{3},q_{3},e_{3}),(V_{3},q_{3},e_{3}),(V_{3},q_{3},e_{3}),\circledast_{3}\bigr)

where

(2.32)

x\circledast_{3}y=(e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x})*_{3}(\overline{y}*_{2}e_{1})\qquad\text{for $x$, $y\in V_{3}$.}

We also define linear maps
\Delta_{1}\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
,

\Delta_{2}\colon V_{2}\to V_{3}
,
\Delta_{3}\colon V_{3}\to V_{1}
as follows:
for
x_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
and
x_{3}\in V_{3}
,

\Delta_{1}(x_{1})=e_{3}*_{2}\overline{x_{1}},\qquad\Delta_{2}(x_{2})=e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}},\qquad\Delta_{3}(x_{3})=e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x_{3}}.

Theorem 2.25
.

With the notation above,

(a)

the triple
\Delta=(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3})
is an isomorphism

\Delta\colon\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}\to\partial\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
;

(b)

the triple
(\Delta_{3},\Delta_{2}^{-1},\operatorname{Id}_{V_{3}})
is an isomorphism

S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})\to\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}
;

(c)

\partial S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})=S(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet})
.

Proof.

(a)
It is clear that each
\Delta_{i}
is an isometry of pointed quadratic
spaces, so it suffices to prove
\Delta_{3}(x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})=\Delta_{1}(x_{1})*_{1}\Delta_{2}(x_{2})
for
x_{1}\in V_{1}
and
x_{2}\in V_{2}
,
which amounts to

e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x_{1}*_{3}x_{2}}=(e_{3}*_{2}\overline{x_{1}})*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}\overline{x_{2}}).

This readily follows from (d) of Lemma
2.24
.

(b)
For
y\in V_{3}
we have
\Delta_{2}(\overline{y}*_{2}e_{1})=e_{1}*_{3}\overline{\overline{y}*_{2}e_{1}}=y
, hence by definition

x\circledast_{3}y=\Delta_{3}(x)*_{3}\Delta_{2}^{-1}(y)
, which proves (b).

(c)
It suffices to prove

b_{3}(x\circledast_{3}y,z)=b_{3}(x,y\circledast_{3}z)\qquad\text{for
$x$, $y$, $z\in V_{3}$.}

For this, we first compute using Lemma
2.24

\displaystyle\overline{y\circledast_{3}z}

\displaystyle=\overline{(e_{2}*_{1}\overline{y})*_{3}(\overline{z}*_{2}e_{1})}

\displaystyle=\bigl((e_{3}*_{2}\overline{e_{2}*_{1}\overline{y}})*_{1}(e_{1}*_{3}\overline{\overline{z}*_{2}e_{1}})\bigr)*_{3}e_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigl(\bigl(e_{3}*_{2}(e_{2}*_{1}y)\bigr)*_{1}\bigl(e_{1}*_{3}(z*_{2}e_{1})\bigr)\bigr)*_{3}e_{2}

\displaystyle=\bigl((\overline{y}*_{2}e_{1})*_{1}z\bigr)*_{3}e_{2}.

Since
\overline{\rule{7.11317pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}}
and multiplication on the left by
e_{2}
are
isometries, it follows that

b_{3}(x,y\circledast_{3}z)=b_{1}(e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x},(\overline{y}*_{2}e_{1})*_{1}z).

By definition of
*_{1}
, the right side is

b_{3}\bigl((e_{2}*_{1}\overline{x})*_{3}(\overline{y}*_{2}e_{1}),z\bigr)=b_{3}(x\circledast_{3}y,z).\qed

For compositions of (unpointed) isometric spaces, a result similar to
Theorem
2.25
easily follows:

Corollary 2.26
.

For every composition of isometric quadratic spaces
\mathcal{C}
, there
is an isomorphism
\mathcal{C}\simeq\partial\mathcal{C}
. Moreover,
\mathcal{C}
is
isomorphic to a composition
S(\mathcal{C})
such that

\partial S(\mathcal{C})=S(\mathcal{C})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\bigl((V_{1},q_{1}),(V_{2},q_{2}),(V_{3},q_{3}),*_{3}\bigr)
be a
composition of isometric quadratic spaces. By
Corollary
2.11
, we know that
q_{1}
,
q_{2}
and
q_{3}

represent
1
. We may therefore use the same constructions as in
Theorem
2.25
, after choosing
e_{1}\in V_{1}
and
e_{2}\in V_{2}
such that

q_{1}(e_{1})=q_{2}(e_{2})=1
and letting
e_{3}=e_{1}*_{3}e_{2}
, for then

\bigl((V_{1},q_{1},e_{1}),(V_{2},q_{2},e_{2}),(V_{3},q_{3},e_{3}),*_{3}\bigr)
is a
composition of pointed quadratic spaces. Define the maps

\Delta_{1}\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
,
\Delta_{2}\colon V_{2}\to V_{3}
,

\Delta_{3}\colon V_{3}\to V_{1}
as in Theorem
2.25
. The proof
of that theorem shows that
(\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},\Delta_{3})
is an
isomorphism
\mathcal{C}\to\partial\mathcal{C}
. Moreover, letting

S(\mathcal{C})=\bigl((V_{3},q_{3}),(V_{3},q_{3}),(V_{3},q_{3}),\circledast_{3}\bigr)

with
\circledast_{3}
as in (
2.32
), we see from the
proof of Theorem
2.25
that
\partial S(\mathcal{C})=S(\mathcal{C})

and that
(\Delta_{3},\Delta_{2}^{-1},\operatorname{Id}_{V_{3}})
is an isomorphism

S(\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Note that, in contrast with Theorem
2.25
, the constructions
in Corollary
2.26
are not canonical, since they depend on
the choice of distinguished vectors.

2.5.
Composition algebras

The purpose of this subsection is to briefly review the classical notion
of composition algebra, in order to underline its connections with compositions
of quadratic spaces.

Definitions 2.27
.

A
composition algebra
over
F
is a triple

\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond)

where

(A,q)
is a (finite-dimensional) quadratic space over
F
with
nonsingular polar bilinear form and
\diamond\colon A\times A\to A

is a bilinear map such that

q(x\diamond y)=q(x)q(y)\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y\in A$.}

The definition can be rephrased as follows: the
4
-tuple

(2.33)

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})=\bigl((A,q),\,(A,q),\,(A,q),\,\diamond\bigr)

is a composition of quadratic spaces.
Theorem
2.6
shows that the dimension of a composition
algebra is
1
,
2
,
4
or
8
, with dimension
1
occurring only
when
\charac F\neq 2
, and Corollary
2.11
shows that

q
is a Pfister form.

A
unital composition algebra
5
5

5

Unital composition
algebras are called

Hurwitz algebras
in
[
13
]
, see
[
13
, (33.17)]
.

is a
4
-tuple

\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}=(A,q,e,\diamond)
where

(A,q,e)
is a pointed quadratic space and
\diamond\colon A\times A\to A
is a bilinear map such that

q(x\diamond y)=q(x)q(y)\qquad\text{and}\qquad e\diamond x=x\diamond e=x\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y\in A$.}

In any unital composition algebra we have
e\diamond e=e
, hence

(2.34)

\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})=\bigl((A,q,e),\,(A,q,e),\,(A,q,e),\,\diamond\bigr)

is a composition of pointed quadratic spaces.

As with more general compositions of quadratic spaces, the
multiplication law

\diamond
of a composition algebra
\mathcal{A}
induces derived
composition maps
\diamond_{1}
and
\diamond_{2}
of
(A,q)
,
(A,q)
,

(A,q)
, defined by the conditions

(2.35)

b(x,y\diamond_{1}z)=b(x\diamond y,z)\qquad\text{and}\qquad b(x\diamond_{2}y,z)=b(x,y\diamond z)\qquad\text{for $x$, $y$,
$z\in A$.}

We may therefore define derived composition algebras
\partial\mathcal{A}

and
\partial^{2}\mathcal{A}
by

\partial\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond_{1})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\partial^{2}\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond_{2}).

Composition algebras
\mathcal{A}
such that

\partial\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}
are called
symmetric
composition algebras
. They are characterized by the condition that

b(x\diamond y,z)=b(x,y\diamond z)\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y$,
$z\in A$.}

By contrast with compositions of pointed quadratic spaces, the
derivation procedure does
not
preserve unitality of composition
algebras. To make this point clear, we determine below the derived
composition maps of a unital composition algebra, using results
from
[
19
, Ch. 1]
. Note that unital composition algebras carry a
canonical involutory isometry
\overline{\rule{7.11317pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}}

derived from their pointed quadratic space structure as
in Definitions
2.22
.

Proposition 2.28
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}=(A,q,e,\diamond)
be a unital composition algebra.

(a)

The derived composition maps
\diamond_{1}
and
\diamond_{2}
defined
in (
2.35
) are given by

x\diamond_{1}y=y\diamond\overline{x}\qquad\text{and}\qquad x\diamond_{2}y=\overline{y}\diamond x\qquad\text{for $x$, $y\in A$.}

(b)

For the bilinear map
*\colon A\times A\to A
defined by

x*y=\overline{x}\diamond\overline{y}\qquad\text{for $x$, $y\in A$,}

the triple
S(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})=(A,q,*)
is a symmetric composition
algebra.

Proof.

(a)
Lemma 1.3.2 in
[
19
]
yields
b(x,y\diamond z)=b(z,\overline{y}\diamond x)
, hence
b(x\diamond_{2}y,z)=b(\overline{y}\diamond x,z)

for all
x
,
y
,
z\in A
. Since
b
is nonsingular, it follows
that
x\diamond_{2}y=\overline{y}\diamond x
.

Since
\overline{\rule{7.11317pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}}
is an isometry,
[
19
, Lemma 1.3.2]
also yields

b(\overline{x\diamond y},\overline{z})=b(\overline{y},\overline{\overline{x}\diamond z})
. Now,
[
19
, Lemma1.3.1]

shows that
\overline{x\diamond y}=\overline{y}\diamond\overline{x}
,
hence the definition of
\diamond_{1}
yields

b(\overline{y}\diamond\overline{x},\overline{z})=b(\overline{y},\overline{z}\diamond x)=b(\overline{z},x\diamond_{1}\overline{y})\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y$, $z\in A$.}

Since
b
is nonsingular, it follows that
x\diamond_{1}\overline{y}=\overline{y}\diamond\overline{x}
.

(b)
Since
q(\overline{x})=q(x)
for all
x\in A
, it is clear that

S(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})
is a composition algebra. To prove
that the derived maps

*_{1}
,
*_{2}
associated to
*
are identical to
*
, it suffices to
prove that
b(x*y,z)=b(x,y*z)
for all
x
,
y
,
z\in A
, which
amounts to

(2.36)

b(\overline{x}\diamond\overline{y},z)=b(x,\overline{y}\diamond\overline{z})\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y$, $z\in A$.}

From the definition of
\diamond_{1}
in (
2.35
) and its
determination in (a), it follows that

b(\overline{x}\diamond\overline{y},z)=b(\overline{x},\overline{y}\diamond_{1}z)=b(\overline{x},z\diamond y)\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y$, $z\in A$.}

Now,
z\diamond y=\overline{\overline{y}\diamond\overline{z}}

by
[
19
, Lemma 1.3.1]
, and
\overline{\rule{7.11317pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}}
is an isometry, hence the
rightmost side in the last displayed equation is equal to

b(x,\overline{y}\diamond\overline{z})
, which
proves (
2.36
).
∎

Note that in the context of Proposition
2.28
,

x\diamond_{1}e=\overline{x}=e\diamond_{2}x
for all
x\in A
.
Hence
(A,q,e,\diamond_{1})
and
(A,q,e,\diamond_{2})
are
not

unital composition algebras, unless
\overline{\rule{7.11317pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{4.0pt}}=\operatorname{Id}_{V}
, which occurs only
if
\dim A=1
.

Symmetric composition algebras
S(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})
derived from unital
composition algebras
\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}
as in
Proposition
2.28
are called
para-unital

composition algebras (
para-Hurwitz algebras
in the terminology of

[
13
]
). They are characterized by the property that they contain
a
para-unit
, see
[
13
, (34.8)]
.

Between algebras, maps that are more general than homomorphisms are
considered, following Albert
[
1
]
.

Definition 2.29
.

Let
(A,\diamond)
and
(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\diamond})
be
F
-algebras
(i.e.,
F
-vector spaces with a bilinear multiplication). An

isotopy

f\colon(A,\diamond)\to(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\diamond})
is
a triple
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
of linear bijections
f_{i}\colon A\to\widetilde{A}
such that

f_{3}(x\diamond y)=f_{1}(x)\,\widetilde{\diamond}\,f_{2}(y)\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y\in A$.}

An
autotopy
is an isotopy of an algebra to itself. Under the
composition of maps, autotopies of an algebra form a group

\Str(A,\diamond)
known as the
structure group
of

(A,\diamond)
. This group is the set of
F
-rational points of an
algebraic group scheme
\BStr(A,\diamond)
, which is a closed
subgroup of
\BGL(A)\times\BGL(A)\times\BGL(A)
.

For example, in the construction
S
of
Proposition
2.28
, which yields the symmetric
composition algebra
S(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})
from the unital composition
algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}
, the algebra
(A,*)
is isotopic to

(A,\diamond)
. The following construction, due to Kaplansky
[
11
]
,
shows that the algebra of every composition algebra is isotopic to the
algebra of a unital composition algebra.

Proposition 2.30
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond)
be a composition algebra. There exists a
bilinear map
*\colon A\times A\to A
and a vector
e\in A
for
which

(a)

(A,q,e,*)
is a unital composition algebra, and

(b)

there exists
an isotopy
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\colon(A,\diamond)\to(A,*)
which is
also an isomorphism
f\colon\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{C}(A,q,*)
of the
associated
compositions of quadratic spaces as in (
2.33
).

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{A}
is a composition algebra,
Corollary
2.11
shows
that there exists
u\in A
such that
q(u)=1
. The maps

\ell_{u}\colon A\to A
and
r_{u}\colon A\to A
defined by

\ell_{u}(x)=u\diamond x
and
r_{u}(x)=x\diamond u
are isometries of

(A,q)
, hence they are invertible. Define

*\colon A\times A\to A
by

x*y=r_{u}^{-1}(x)\diamond\ell_{u}^{-1}(y)\qquad\text{for $x$, $y\in A$},

hence
x\diamond y=r_{u}(x)*\ell_{u}(y)
for
x
,
y\in A
.
It is clear from the definitions that
(\operatorname{Id}_{A},r_{u},\ell_{u})

is an isotopy
(A,\diamond)\to(A,*)
and also a similitude

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{C}(A,q,*)
with multiplier of the form

(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},1)
for some
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}\in F^{\times}
. Since
\mu(\ell_{u})=\mu(r_{u})=q(u)=1
, it follows
from (
2.25
) that
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=1
, hence
f
is
an isomorphism of compositions of quadratic spaces. Moreover for

e=u\diamond u
we have
r_{u}^{-1}(e)=u=\ell_{u}^{-1}(e)
, hence

e*x=u\diamond\ell_{u}^{-1}(x)=\ell_{u}\bigl(\ell_{u}^{-1}(x)\bigr)=x=r_{u}\bigl(r_{u}^{-1}(x)\bigr)=r_{u}^{-1}(x)\diamond u=x*e,

hence
(A,q,e,*)
is a unital composition algebra.
∎

Corollary 2.31
.

Every composition of isometric quadratic spaces is isomorphic to a
composition
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
as in (
2.33
) for some unital
composition algebra
\mathcal{A}
, and also to a composition

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})
for some symmetric composition algebra

\mathcal{S}
. Up to isomorphism, there is a unique composition of
hyperbolic quadratic spaces of dimension
n
, for
n=2
,
4
and
8
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\bigl((V_{1},q_{1}),(V_{2},q_{2}),(V_{3},q_{3}),*_{3}\bigr)
be a
composition of isometric quadratic spaces, and let

S(\mathcal{C})=\bigl((V_{3},q_{3}),(V_{3},q_{3}),(V_{3},q_{3}),\circledast_{3}\bigr)

be the composition of quadratic spaces constructed in
Corollary
2.26
, which is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}
. Clearly,

S(\mathcal{C})=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})
for
\mathcal{S}
the composition
algebra
\mathcal{S}=(V_{3},q_{3},\circledast_{3})
. This composition
algebra is symmetric since
\partial S(\mathcal{C})=S(\mathcal{C})
. Now,
Proposition
2.30

yields a unital composition algebra
\mathcal{A}=(V_{3},q_{3},e,*)
and an
isomorphism
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})\simeq\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
. Thus,
\mathcal{C}
is
isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{S})
and to
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
.

For
n=2
,
4
and
8
, there is up to isomorphism a unique unital
composition algebra of dimension
n
with hyperbolic quadratic form,
namely the split quadratic étale algebra, the split quaternion
algebra and the split octonion algebra,
see
[
13
, (33.19)]
. Therefore, there is a unique composition of
hyperbolic quadratic spaces up to isomorphism.
∎

By contrast with the last argument, note that there exist more than one
symmetric composition algebra of dimension
8
with hyperbolic
quadratic form, see
[
13
, (34.37)]
; but their associated
compositions of quadratic spaces are isomorphic.

We next relate isotopies of composition algebras with similitudes of
the associated compositions of quadratic spaces.

Theorem 2.32
.

Let
\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{q},\widetilde{\diamond})
be composition algebras and let
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
and

\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
be the associated compositions of quadratic
spaces as in (
2.33
). A triple
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
of
bijective linear maps
f_{i}\colon A\to\widetilde{A}
is an isotopy

(A,\diamond)\to(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\diamond})
if and only if it is a
similitude
f\colon\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
with
composition multiplier
(\mu(f_{2}),\mu(f_{1}),1)
.

Proof.

It is clear by comparing Definitions
2.12
and

2.29
that
similitudes
f\colon\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
with
composition multiplier of the form
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},1)
for
some
\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}\in F^{\times}
are isotopies

(A,\diamond)\to(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\diamond})
. To prove the converse, it
suffices to show that for every isotopy
f=(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\colon(A,\diamond)\to(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\diamond})
the maps
f_{i}
are
similitudes. The isotopy condition then shows that
f
is a
similitude of compositions
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{\mathcal{A}})
with
multiplier of the form
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},1)
for some

\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}\in F^{\times}
. Then (
2.25
) shows
that
\lambda_{1}=\mu(f_{2})
and
\lambda_{2}=\mu(f_{1})
.

By Proposition
2.30
we can find a bilinear map
*\colon A\times A\to A
and a vector
e\in A
such that
(A,q,e,*)
is a
unital composition algebra and there exists an isotopy

g\colon(A,\diamond)\to(A,*)
whose components are isometries of

(A,q)
. The map
f\circ g^{-1}\colon(A,*)\to(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{\diamond})
is then an isotopy, which means that for
f\circ g^{-1}=(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})

(2.37)

h_{3}(x*y)=h_{1}(x)\,\widetilde{\diamond}\,h_{2}(y)\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y\in A$.}

In particular, it follows that for all
x\in A

h_{1}(e)\,\widetilde{\diamond}\,h_{2}(x)=h_{3}(e*x)=h_{3}(x)=h_{3}(x*e)=h_{1}(x)\,\widetilde{\diamond}\,h_{2}(e).

Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is a composition algebra, these equations imply

(2.38)

\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(x)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(e)\bigr)\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(x)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(x)\bigr)\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(e)\bigr)\qquad\text{for all $x\in A$.}

As
h_{3}
is bijective, there exist vectors
x\in A
such that

\widetilde{q}\bigl(h(x)\bigr)\neq 0
, hence
\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(e)\bigr)\neq 0
and
\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(e)\bigr)\neq 0
. Equation (
2.38
) then yields

\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(e)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(e)\bigr)\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(e)\bigr)\neq 0
. Define
q^{\prime}\colon A\to F
by

q^{\prime}(x)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(e)\bigr)^{-1}\widetilde{q}\bigl(h(x)\bigr)\qquad\text{for $x\in A$,}

so
q^{\prime}(e)=1
. Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
is a composition algebra, we
obtain from (
2.37
)

\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(x*y)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(x)\bigr)\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(y)\bigr)\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y\in A$,}

hence by (
2.38
)

\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(x*y)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(e)\bigr)^{-1}\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(e)\bigr)^{-1}\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(x)\bigr)\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(y)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(e)\bigr)^{-1}\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(x)\bigr)\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(y)\bigr).

Therefore,

q^{\prime}(x*y)=q^{\prime}(x)q^{\prime}(y)\qquad\text{for all $x$, $y\in A$.}

Thus,
(A,q^{\prime},e,*)
is a unital composition algebra, just like

(A,q,e,*)
. But the quadratic form in a unital composition algebra
is uniquely determined as the “generic norm” of the algebra
(see
[
13
, (33.9)]
or
[
19
, Cor. 1.2.4]
), hence
q^{\prime}=q
,
which means that

\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(x)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(e)\bigr)q(x)\qquad\text{for all $x\in A$.}

Thus,
h_{3}\colon(A,q)\to(\widetilde{A},\widetilde{q})
is a similitude with
multiplier
\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(e)\bigr)
. Equation (
2.38
)
then yields

\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(x)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(e)\bigr)^{-1}\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(e)\bigr)q(x)\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{2}(x)\bigr)=\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{1}(e)\bigr)^{-1}\widetilde{q}\bigl(h_{3}(e)\bigr)q(x),

hence
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
also are similitudes. Now,
f=h\circ g
and
all the components of
g
are isometries, hence all the components
of
f
are similitudes.
∎

In the case where
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{A}
Theorem
2.32
has the
following immediate consequence:

Corollary 2.33
.

For any composition algebra
\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond)
, let

\lambda^{\prime}\colon\BGO\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}
be the third
component of the composition multiplier map

\lambda_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})}\colon\BGO\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}^{3}
, and let
\mu^{\prime}\colon\BStr(A,\diamond)\to\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}^{2}
be the map defined on rational points
by mapping every autotopy
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
to the pair of multipliers

\bigl(\mu(f_{1}),\mu(f_{2})\bigr)
. The algebraic group scheme

\BStr(A,\diamond)
is smooth and fits in the following exact
sequences:

1\to\BStr(A,\diamond)\to\BGO\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)\xrightarrow{\lambda^{\prime}}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}\to 1

and

1\to\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)\to\BStr(A,\diamond)\xrightarrow{\mu^{\prime}}\mathbf{G}_{\mathbf{m}}^{2}\to 1.

Proof.

Theorem
2.32
identifies
\BStr(A,\diamond)
as the kernel
of
\lambda^{\prime}
. Proposition
2.18
shows that

\lambda_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})}
is a separable morphism, hence
\lambda^{\prime}

also is separable, and it follows by
[
13
, (22.13)]
that

\BStr(A,\diamond)
is smooth. Theorem
2.32
also shows
that the kernel of
\mu^{\prime}
is the kernel of
the restriction of
\lambda_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})}
to

\BStr(A,\diamond)
, which is
\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
by
definition. To complete the proof, observe that
\mu^{\prime}
is surjective
because
\lambda_{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})}
is surjective.
∎

We next turn to automorphisms of an algebra
(A,\diamond)
, which are
linear bijections
f\colon A\to A
such that
f(x\diamond y)=f(x)\diamond f(y)
for all
x
,
y\in A
. They form an algebraic
group scheme
\BAut(A,\diamond)
, which can be viewed as a closed
subgroup of
\BStr(A,\diamond)
since every automorphism
f
yields an
autotopy
(f,f,f)
of
(A,\diamond)
. To relate the condition that

f_{1}=f_{2}=f_{3}
in an autotopy
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})
with the shift
isomorphism
\partial\colon\BGO\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)\to\BGO\bigl(\partial\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
, we view

\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
,

\BOrth\bigl(\partial\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
and

\BOrth\bigl(\partial^{2}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr))
as subgroups of

\BGL(A)\times\BGL(A)\times\BGL(A)
and define

\overline{\BOrth}\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)=\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)\cap\BOrth\bigl(\partial\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)\cap\BOrth\bigl(\partial^{2}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr).

The shift isomorphism
\partial
clearly restricts to an automorphism
of
\overline{\BOrth}\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
.

Proposition 2.34
.

For every composition algebra
\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond)
, the group

\BAut(A,\diamond)
is the subgroup of

\overline{\BOrth}\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
fixed under
\partial
.

Proof.

Let
R
be a commutative
F
-algebra. For every automorphism
f
of

(A,\diamond)_{R}
the triple
(f,f,f)
is an autotopy of

(A,\diamond)_{R}
, hence by Theorem
2.32
a similitude of

\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})_{R}
with composition multiplier

(\mu(f),\mu(f),1)
. The relations (
2.25
) between
composition multipliers and the multipliers of the components of
similitudes yield
\mu(f)=\mu(f)^{2}
, hence
\mu(f)=1
and
(f,f,f)

is an automorphism of
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})_{R}
. By Proposition
2.13
we
then see that
(f,f,f)
is also an automorphism of

\partial\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
and of
\partial^{2}\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
, hence

(f,f,f)\in\overline{\BOrth}\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)(R)
, and this
triple is fixed under
\partial
. On the other hand, if

(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\in\overline{\BOrth}\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)(R)
is
fixed under
\partial
, then
f_{1}=f_{2}=f_{3}
, hence
f_{1}
is an
automorphism of
(A,\diamond)_{R}
because
(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\in\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)(R)
.
∎

When
\mathcal{A}=(A,q,\diamond)
is a symmetric composition algebra, then

\partial\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})
by definition, hence

\overline{\BOrth}\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)=\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
and Proposition
2.34

shows that
\BAut(A,\diamond)
is the subgroup of

\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{A})\bigr)
fixed under
\partial
. When
\dim A=8
, an alternative description is given
in
[
6
, Th. 6.6]
:
\BAut(A,\diamond)
is shown to be isomorphic
to the subgroup of
\BPGO^{+}(q)
fixed under an outer automorphism of
order
3
, which is the analogue of
\partial
.

For a unital composition algebra

\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}=(A,q,e,\diamond)
with associated para-unital symmetric
composition algebra
S(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})=(A,q,*)
as in
Proposition
2.28
, it follows from functoriality of
the
S
construction that
\BAut(A,\diamond)\subset\BAut(A,*)
. The
reverse inclusion holds when
\dim A\geq 4

by
[
13
, (34.4)]
. However, the group
\BAut(A,\diamond)
can also
be described as follows:

Proposition 2.35
.

Let
\mathcal{A}^{\bullet}=(A,q,e,\diamond)
be a unital composition
algebra, and let
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})
be the associated
composition of pointed quadratic spaces as
in (
2.34
). There is a canonical identification

\BAut(A,\diamond)=\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})\bigr)
.

Proof.

Let
R
be a commutative
F
-algebra. Every automorphism

f\in\BAut(A,\diamond)(R)
leaves
e
fixed, hence the triple

(f,f,f)
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})_{R}
.
Therefore, mapping
f
to
(f,f,f)
defines an embedding

\BAut(A,\diamond)\subset\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})\bigr)
.

For the reverse inclusion, let

(f_{1},f_{2},f_{3})\in\BOrth\bigl(\mathcal{C}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}^{\bullet})\bigr)(R)
.
Substituting
e
for
x
or for
y
in the
equation

f_{3}(x\diamond y)=f_{1}(x)\diamond f_{2}(y)\qquad\text{for all $x$,
$y\in A$}

yields
f_{3}(y)=f_{2}(y)
and
f_{3}(x)=f_{1}(x)
for all
x
,
y\in A
,
hence
f_{3}\in\BAut(A,\diamond)(R)
.
∎

3.
Trialitarian triples

The focus in this section is on central simple algebras with quadratic
pair of degree
8
over an arbitrary field
F
. Altering slightly the
definition in
[
13
, §42.A]
(and extending it to
characteristic
2
), we define a
trialitarian
triple
over
F
to be a
4
-tuple (!)

\mathcal{T}=\bigl((A_{1},\sigma_{1},\strf_{1}),\,(A_{2},\sigma_{2},\strf_{2}),\,(A_{3},\sigma_{3},\strf_{3}),\,\varphi_{0}\bigr)

where
(A_{i},\sigma_{i},\strf_{i})
is a central simple
F
-algebra with
quadratic pair of degree
8
for
i=1
,
2
,
3
, and
\varphi_{0}
is
an isomorphism of algebras with quadratic pair

\varphi_{0}\colon(C(A_{1},\sigma_{1},\strf_{1}),\underline{\sigma}_{1},\underline{\strf}_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(A_{2},\sigma_{2},\strf_{2})\times(A_{3},\sigma_{3},\strf_{3}).

To simplify notation, we denote by a single letter algebras with
quadratic pair, as in Section
1
, and write

\mathfrak{A}_{i}=(A_{i},\sigma_{i},\strf_{i})
and

\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A}_{1})=(C(\mathfrak{A}_{1}),\underline{\sigma}_{1},\underline{\strf}_{1})
.

If
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}=(\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{1},\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{2},\,\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{3},\,\widetilde{\varphi}_{0})
is also a trialitarian triple,
an
isomorphism of trialitarian triples

\gamma\colon\mathcal{T}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
is a triple
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
of isomorphisms of algebras with quadratic pair

\gamma_{i}\colon\mathfrak{A}_{i}\to\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}\qquad i=1,2,3,

such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{C(\gamma_{1})}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{A}_{2}\times\mathfrak{A}_{3}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{2}\times\gamma_{3}}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{C}(\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{2}\times\widetilde{\mathfrak{A}}_{3}}

We show in §
3.1
that every composition
\mathcal{C}
of
quadratic spaces of dimension
8
yields a trialitarian triple

\End(\mathcal{C})
, and that every trialitarian triple of split algebras
has the form
\End(\mathcal{C})
for some composition
\mathcal{C}
of
dimension
8
. In §
3.2
we discuss the group scheme of
automorphisms
of a trialitarian triple
\mathcal{T}
: we
show that
\BAut(\mathcal{T})
is smooth (hence an algebraic group) and
introduce algebraic groups
\BOrth(\mathcal{T})
,
\BGO(\mathcal{T})
,
\BPGO(\mathcal{T})
,
extending to the context of trialitarian triples the group schemes

\BOrth(\mathcal{C})
,
\BGO(\mathcal{C})
,
\BPGO(\mathcal{C})
defined
in §
2.3
for a composition
\mathcal{C}
of quadratic
spaces. A main result of the section is the construction of derived
trialitarian triples in §
3.3
: to each trialitarian
triple
\mathcal{T}
we canonically associate trialitarian triples

\partial\mathcal{T}
and
\partial^{2}\mathcal{T}
, in such a way that for split
trialitarian triples
\End(\mathcal{C})

\partial\End(\mathcal{C})=\End(\partial\mathcal{C})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\partial^{2}\End(\mathcal{C})=\End(\partial^{2}\mathcal{C}).

This construction is used in §
3.4
to define for each
trialitarian triple
\mathcal{T}=(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\mathfrak{A}_{2},\mathfrak{A}_{3},\varphi_{0})

isomorphisms

\BOrth(\mathcal{T})\simeq\BSpin(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\simeq\BSpin(\mathfrak{A}_{2})\simeq\BSpin(\mathfrak{A}_{3})

and

\BPGO(\mathcal{T})\simeq\BPGO^{+}(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\simeq\BPGO^{+}(\mathfrak{A}_{2})\simeq\BPGO^{+}(\mathfrak{A}_{3}),

which we call the
trialitarian isomorphisms
canonically
attached to the trialitarian triple
\mathcal{T}
.

We return in §
3.5
to the study of compositions of
quadratic spaces, building on the theory of trialitarian triples
developed in the previous subsections to obtain a few more results
about the

8
-dimensional case. Specifically, we establish criteria for the
similarity or the isomorphism of compositions of quadratic spaces,
which yield an analogue of the classical Principle of Triality, and we give an explicit description of the cohomological invariants of

\BSpin_{8}
.

In the final §
3.6
we show that the constructions of
§
3.2
readily yield a canonical isomorphism between the
structure group of a composition algebra of dimension
8
and the
extended Clifford group of its quadratic form.

3.1.
The trialitarian triple of a composition of quadratic
spaces

Let

\mathcal{C}=\bigl((V_{1},q_{1}),\,(V_{2},q_{2}),\,(V_{3},q_{3}),\,*_{3}\bigr)

be a composition of quadratic spaces of dimension
8
over
F
. Recall
from Proposition
2.9
the isomorphism of algebras with
quadratic pair

C(\alpha)\colon(C(V_{1},q_{1}),\tau_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{1})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3}),\sigma_{b_{2}\perp b_{3}},\strf_{q_{2}\perp q_{3}})

induced by the map

\alpha\colon x_{1}\in V_{1}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{x_{1}}\\
\ell_{x_{1}}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})

where
\ell_{x_{1}}\colon V_{2}\to V_{3}
carries
x_{2}\in V_{2}
to

x_{1}*_{3}x_{2}\in V_{3}
and
r_{x_{1}}\colon V_{3}\to V_{2}
carries
x_{3}\in V_{3}

to
x_{3}*_{2}x_{1}\in V_{2}
. Its restriction to the even Clifford algebra
also is an isomorphism of algebras with quadratic pair

C_{0}(\alpha)\colon(C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1}),\tau_{01},\mathfrak{g}_{01})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\End(V_{2}),\sigma_{b_{2}},\strf_{q_{2}})\times(\End(V_{3}),\sigma_{b_{3}},\strf_{q_{3}}),

see Proposition
2.9
. Therefore, the following is a
trialitarian triple:

\End(\mathcal{C})=\bigl((\End(V_{1}),\sigma_{b_{1}},\strf_{q_{1}}),\,(\End(V_{2}),\sigma_{b_{2}},\strf_{q_{2}}),\,(\End(V_{3}),\sigma_{b_{3}},\strf_{q_{3}}),\,C_{0}(\alpha)\bigr).

We next show that the construction of trialitarian
triples from compositions of quadratic spaces is functorial.

Let
\mathcal{C}=\bigl((V_{1},q_{1}),\,(V_{2},q_{2}),\,(V_{3},q_{3}),\,*_{3}\bigr)
and

\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}=\bigl((\widetilde{V}_{1},\widetilde{q}_{1}),\,(\widetilde{V}_{2},\widetilde{q}_{2}),\,(\widetilde{V}_{3},\widetilde{q}_{3}),\,\widetilde{*}_{3}\bigr)
be
compositions of quadratic spaces of dimension
8
. Recall that for
every linear isomorphism
g_{i}\colon V_{i}\to\widetilde{V}_{i}
, we define

\Int(g_{i})\colon\End(V_{i})\to\End(\widetilde{V}_{i})\qquad\text{by $f\mapsto g_{i}\circ f\circ g_{i}^{-1}$.}

Proposition 3.1
.

For every similitude
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\colon\mathcal{C}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
, the triple

\Int(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}):=\bigl(\Int(g_{1}),\Int(g_{2}),\Int(g_{3})\bigr)\colon\End(\mathcal{C})\to\End(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}})

is an isomorphism of trialitarian triples.
Moreover, for every isomorphism of trialitarian triples

(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\colon\End(\mathcal{C})\to\End(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}})
,
there exists a similitude
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\colon\mathcal{C}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
such
that

(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})=\Int(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}).

Proof.

Suppose that
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\colon\mathcal{C}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
is a
similitude. For
i=1
,
2
,
3
,
\Int(g_{i})

is an isomorphism of algebras with quadratic pairs

\Int(g_{i})\colon(\End(V_{i}),\sigma_{b_{i}},\strf_{q_{i}})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\End(\widetilde{V}_{i}),\sigma_{\widetilde{b}_{i}},\strf_{\widetilde{q}_{i}}).

Note that under the identification

C(\End(V_{1}),\sigma_{b_{1}},\strf_{q_{1}})=C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})
the
isomorphism

C\bigl(\Int(g_{1})\bigr)
induced by
\Int(g_{1})
is the isomorphism

C_{0}(g_{1})\colon C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})\to C_{0}(\widetilde{V}_{1},\widetilde{q}_{1})
that
maps
x_{1}y_{1}

to
\mu_{1}^{-1}g_{1}(x_{1})g_{1}(y_{1})
for
x_{1}
,
y_{1}\in V_{1}
, where

\mu_{1}
is the multiplier of
g_{1}
. Therefore,
in order to show that
\bigl(\Int(g_{1}),\Int(g_{2}),\Int(g_{3})\bigr)
is
an
isomorphism of trialitarian triples
\End(\mathcal{C})\to\End(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}})
, we
have to show that the following diagram commutes:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 30.43413pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.21077pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-30.43413pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{C_{0}(g_{1})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.42197pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\alpha)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.21077pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.21077pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.69577pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.54166pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Int(g_{2})\times\Int(g_{3})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 97.69577pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-24.4938pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C_{0}(\widetilde{V}_{1},\widetilde{q}_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.63507pt\raise-24.51389pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.73611pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{C_{0}(\widetilde{\alpha})}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.71075pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 52.71075pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\End(\widetilde{V}_{2})\times\End(\widetilde{V}_{3})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Let
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3})
be the
composition
multiplier of
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
. For
x_{1}
,
y_{1}\in V_{1}
we have

C_{0}(\widetilde{\alpha})\circ C_{0}(g_{1})(x_{1}\cdot y_{1})=\mu_{1}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}r_{g_{1}(x_{1})}\ell_{g_{1}(y_{1})}&0\\
0&\ell_{g_{1}(x_{1})}r_{g_{1}(y_{1})}\end{pmatrix}

and

\bigl(\Int(g_{2})\times\Int(g_{3})\bigr)\circ C_{0}(\alpha)(x_{1}\cdot y_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}g_{2}r_{x_{1}}\ell_{y_{1}}g_{2}^{-1}&0\\
0&g_{3}\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}g_{3}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

By Proposition
2.13
,
(g_{3},g_{1},g_{2})
is a similitude
of
\partial^{2}\mathcal{C}
, hence for
x_{1}\in V_{1}
and
x_{2}\in V_{2}

g_{2}r_{x_{1}}\ell_{y_{1}}(x_{2})=g_{2}\bigl((y_{1}*_{3}x_{2})*_{2}x_{1}\bigr)=\\
\lambda_{2}^{-1}g_{3}(y_{1}*_{3}x_{2})*_{2}g_{1}(x_{1})=\lambda_{2}^{-1}\lambda_{3}^{-1}\bigl(g_{1}(y_{1})*_{3}g_{2}(x_{2})\bigr)*_{2}g_{1}(x_{1}).

Since
\lambda_{2}\lambda_{3}=\mu_{1}
by (
2.25
), it follows
that

g_{2}r_{x_{1}}\ell_{y_{1}}=\mu_{1}^{-1}r_{g_{1}(x_{1})}\ell_{g_{1}(y_{1})}g_{2}
. Similarly,

g_{3}\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}=\ell_{g_{1}(x_{1})}r_{g_{1}(y_{1})}g_{3}
, hence
diagram (
3.1
) commutes. The first part of the
proposition is thus proved.

Now, assume

(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\colon\End(\mathcal{C})\to\End(\widetilde{\mathcal{C}})
is an
isomorphism of trialitarian triples. Each
\gamma_{i}
is an
isomorphism

\gamma_{i}\colon(\End(V_{i}),\sigma_{b_{i}},\strf_{q_{i}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\End(\widetilde{V}_{i}),\sigma_{\widetilde{b}_{i}},\strf_{\widetilde{q}_{i}});

Proposition
1.2
shows that

\gamma_{i}=\Int(g_{i})
for some similitude
g_{i}\colon(V_{i},q_{i})\to(\widetilde{V}_{i},\widetilde{q}_{i})
. We
may then also consider the isomorphism

\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&0\\
0&g_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\colon\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})\to\End(\widetilde{V}_{2}\oplus\widetilde{V}_{3}),

which makes the following diagram, where the vertical maps are the
diagonal embeddings, commute:

\textstyle{\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Int(g_{2})\times\Int(g_{3})}
\textstyle{\End(\widetilde{V}_{2})\times\End(\widetilde{V}_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&0\\
0&g_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)}
\textstyle{\End(\widetilde{V}_{2}\oplus\widetilde{V}_{3})}

From the hypothesis that
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})
is an
isomorphism of trialitarian triples, it follows that the
diagram (
3.1
) commutes. Write
\mu_{1}
for the multiplier
of
g_{1}
and consider the linear map

\beta\colon V_{1}\to\End(\widetilde{V}_{2}\oplus\widetilde{V}_{3}),\qquad x_{1}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{g_{1}(x_{1})}\\
\mu_{1}^{-1}\ell_{g_{1}(x_{1})}&0\end{pmatrix}.

For
\widetilde{x}_{2}\in\widetilde{V}_{2}
and
\widetilde{x}_{3}\in\widetilde{V}_{3}
, we
have by (
2.10
) and (
2.12
)

\mu_{1}^{-1}(g_{1}(x_{1})\,\widetilde{*}_{3}\,\widetilde{x}_{2})\,\widetilde{*}_{2}\,g_{1}(x_{1})=\mu_{1}^{-1}\widetilde{q}_{1}\bigl(g_{1}(x_{1})\bigr)\widetilde{x}_{2}

and

\mu_{1}^{-1}g_{1}(x_{1})\,\widetilde{*}_{3}\,\bigl(\widetilde{x}_{3}\,\widetilde{*}_{2}\,g_{1}(x_{1})\bigr)=\mu_{1}^{-1}\widetilde{q}_{1}\bigl(g_{1}(x_{1})\bigr)\widetilde{x}_{3}.

Since
\mu_{1}^{-1}\bigl(\widetilde{q}_{1}(g_{1}(x_{1}))\bigr)=q_{1}(x_{1})
, it
follows that

\beta(x_{1})^{2}=q_{1}(x_{1})\operatorname{Id}_{\widetilde{V}_{2}\oplus\widetilde{V}_{3}}
for
x_{1}\in V_{1}
. Therefore,
\beta
induces an
F
-algebra homomorphism

C(\beta)\colon C(V_{1},q_{1})\to\End(\widetilde{V}_{2}\oplus\widetilde{V}_{3}).

Since
C(V_{1},q_{1})
is a simple algebra, dimension count shows that

C(\beta)
is an isomorphism. For
x_{1}
,
y_{1}\in V_{1}
,

C(\beta)(x_{1}y_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}\mu_{1}^{-1}r_{g_{1}(x_{1})}\ell_{g_{1}(y_{1})}&0\\
0&\mu_{1}^{-1}\ell_{g_{1}(x_{1})}r_{g_{1}(y_{1})}\end{pmatrix}=C_{0}(\widetilde{\alpha})\bigl(C_{0}(g_{1})(x_{1}y_{1})\bigr),

hence
C(\beta)\rvert_{C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})}=C_{0}(\widetilde{\alpha})\circ C_{0}(g_{1})
.
Since the diagram (
3.1
) commutes, it follows that

C(\beta)\rvert_{C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})}=\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&0\\
0&g_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\circ C(\alpha)\rvert_{C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})}.

Therefore,
\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&0\\
0&g_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\circ C(\alpha)\circ C(\beta)^{-1}
is an
automorphism of
\End(\widetilde{V}_{2}\oplus\widetilde{V}_{3})
whose restriction to

C(\beta)\bigl(C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})\bigr)
is the identity. This
automorphism is inner by the Skolem–Noether theorem. Since

C(\beta)\bigl(C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})\bigr)=\End(\widetilde{V}_{2})\times\End(\widetilde{V}_{3})
we
must have

\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&0\\
0&g_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\circ C(\alpha)\circ C(\beta)^{-1}=\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\nu_{2}&0\\
0&\nu_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\qquad\text{for some $\nu_{2}$, $\nu_{3}\in F^{\times}$,}

hence

\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}g_{2}&0\\
0&g_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\circ C(\alpha)(x_{1})=\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\nu_{2}&0\\
0&\nu_{3}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)C(\beta)(x_{1})\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\nu_{2}^{-1}&0\\
0&\nu_{3}^{-1}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\qquad\text{for $x_{1}\in V_{1}$,}

which means that

\begin{pmatrix}0&g_{2}r_{x_{1}}g_{3}^{-1}\\
g_{3}\ell_{x_{1}}g_{2}^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\nu_{2}\nu_{3}^{-1}r_{g_{1}(x_{1})}\\
\nu_{2}^{-1}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}^{-1}\ell_{g_{1}(x_{1})}&0\end{pmatrix}.

The equation

g_{3}\ell_{x_{1}}g_{2}^{-1}=\nu_{2}^{-1}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}^{-1}\ell_{g_{1}(x_{1})}

implies that for
x_{2}\in V_{2}

g_{3}(x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})=\nu_{2}^{-1}\nu_{3}\mu_{1}^{-1}g_{1}(x_{1})\,\widetilde{*}_{3}\,g_{2}(x_{2}).

Therefore,
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
is a similitude

\mathcal{C}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

We next show that every trialitarian triple of split algebras has the
form
\End(\mathcal{C})
for some composition
\mathcal{C}
of quadratic spaces of
dimension
8
.

Theorem 3.2
.

Let
\mathcal{T}=(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\,\mathfrak{A}_{2},\,\mathfrak{A}_{3},\,\varphi_{0})
be a trialitarian
triple over an arbitrary field
F
, where

\mathfrak{A}_{i}=(A_{i},\sigma_{i},\strf_{i})
for
i=1
,
2
,
3
. If
A_{1}
,
A_{2}

and
A_{3}
are split, then
there is a composition
\mathcal{C}
of quadratic spaces of dimension
8

over
F
such that
\mathcal{T}\simeq\End(\mathcal{C})
. The composition
\mathcal{C}

is uniquely determined up to similitude.

Proof.

For
i=1
,
2
,
3
, let
A_{i}=\End(V_{i})
for some
F
-vector space

V_{i}
of dimension
8
. Let also
q_{i}
be a quadratic form on
V_{i}

to which
(\sigma_{i},\strf_{i})
is adjoint. Since
q_{1}
is determined
only
up to a scalar factor, we may assume
q_{1}
represents
1
and pick

e_{1}\in V_{1}
such that
q_{1}(e_{1})=1
. The inner automorphism

\Int(e_{1})
of the full Clifford algebra
C(V_{1},q_{1})
preserves

C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})
and is of order
2
. It transfers under the
isomorphism

C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})=C(\mathfrak{A}_{1})\xrightarrow{\varphi_{0}}A_{2}\times A_{3}=\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})

to an automorphism of
\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})
that interchanges
the two factors. Viewing
\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})
as a subalgebra
diagonally embedded in
\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})
, we may find an inner
automorphism of
\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})
which restricts to

\varphi_{0}\circ\Int(e_{1})\circ\varphi_{0}^{-1}
by (a slight
generalization of) the Skolem–Noether Theorem, see
[
5
, Th. 2,
p. A VIII.252]
. This inner automorphism is conjugation by an
operator of the form
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&u^{\prime}\\
u&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
since it interchanges
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}\operatorname{Id}_{V_{2}}&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
and
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&\operatorname{Id}_{V_{3}}\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
. Since

\varphi_{0}\circ\Int(e_{1})\circ\varphi_{0}^{-1}
has order
2
, it
follows that
uu^{\prime}=u^{\prime}u\in F^{\times}
, hence
\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&u^{-1}\\
u&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
has the same restriction to

\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})
as
\Int\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&u^{\prime}\\
u&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
. Representing
C(V_{1},q_{1})
and

\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})
as (generalized) crossed products

\displaystyle C(V_{1},q_{1})

\displaystyle=C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})\oplus e_{1}C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1}),

\displaystyle\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})

\displaystyle=\bigl(\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})\bigr)\oplus\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&u^{-1}\\
u&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\bigl(\End(V_{2})\times\End(V_{3})\bigr),

we may extend
\varphi_{0}
to an isomorphism of
F
-algebras

\varphi\colon C(V_{1},q_{1})\to\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})

by mapping
e_{1}
to
\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&u^{-1}\\
u&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
. Let
\tau_{1}

be the involution on
C(V_{1},q_{1})
that fixes every vector in
V_{1}

and let

\tau^{\prime}=\varphi\circ\tau_{1}\circ\varphi^{-1}
be the corresponding
involution on
\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})
. The restriction of
\tau_{1}
to

C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})
is the canonical involution
\tau_{01}
, and

\varphi_{0}\circ\tau_{01}=(\sigma_{2}\times\sigma_{3})\circ\varphi_{0}
, hence
\tau^{\prime}
restricts to

\sigma_{2}
and
\sigma_{3}
on
\End(V_{2})
and
\End(V_{3})
. This means
that

(\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3}),\tau^{\prime})\in(\End(V_{2}),\sigma_{2})\boxplus(\End(V_{3}),\sigma_{3}),

i.e., that
\tau^{\prime}
is adjoint to a symmetric bilinear form
that is the orthogonal sum of a multiple of
b_{2}
and a multiple
of
b_{3}
. Scaling
q_{2}
or
q_{3}
, we may assume

\tau^{\prime}=\sigma_{b_{2}\perp b_{3}}
is the adjoint involution of
b_{2}\perp b_{3}
.

Under the isomorphism
\varphi
, the odd part

C_{1}(V_{1},q_{1})=e_{1}C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1})
is mapped to the odd part of

\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})
for the checkerboard grading, hence for each

x_{1}\in V_{1}
there exist
\ell_{x_{1}}\in\Hom(V_{2},V_{3})
and

r_{x_{1}}\in\Hom(V_{3},V_{2})
such that

\varphi(x_{1})=\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{x_{1}}\\
\ell_{x_{1}}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3}).

Since
\tau_{1}(x_{1})=x_{1}
, it follows that
\varphi(x_{1})
is

\sigma_{b_{2}\perp b_{3}}
-symmetric, hence for all
x_{2}
,
y_{2}\in V_{2}

and
x_{3}
,
y_{3}\in V_{3}

(b_{2}\perp b_{3})\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{x_{1}}\\
\ell_{x_{1}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}\\
x_{3}\end{pmatrix},\,\begin{pmatrix}y_{2}\\
y_{3}\end{pmatrix}\right)=(b_{2}\perp b_{3})\left(\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}\\
x_{3}\end{pmatrix},\,\begin{pmatrix}0&r_{x_{1}}\\
\ell_{x_{1}}&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}y_{2}\\
y_{3}\end{pmatrix}\right).

This means that for all
x_{2}
,
y_{2}\in V_{2}
and
x_{3}
,
y_{3}\in V_{3}
,

(3.2)

b_{2}(r_{x_{1}}(x_{3}),y_{2})=b_{3}\bigl(x_{3},\ell_{x_{1}}(y_{2})\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad b_{3}(\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2}),y_{2})=b_{2}\bigl(x_{2},r_{x_{1}}(y_{3})\bigr).

Moreover, the relations
x_{1}^{2}=q_{1}(x_{1})
and

x_{1}y_{1}+y_{1}x_{1}=b_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})
yield for all
x_{1}
,
y_{1}\in V_{1}

(3.3)

\ell_{x_{1}}r_{x_{1}}=r_{x_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}=q_{1}(x_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}+\ell_{y_{1}}r_{x_{1}}=r_{x_{1}}\ell_{y_{1}}+r_{y_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}=b_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}).

Recall that the two components
\varphi_{\pm}
of
\varphi_{0}
are
homomorphisms of algebras with quadratic pair

\varphi_{+}\colon(C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1}),\tau_{01},\underline{\strf}_{1})\to\mathfrak{A}_{2},\qquad\varphi_{-}\colon(C_{0}(V_{1},q_{1}),\tau_{01},\underline{\strf}_{1})\to\mathfrak{A}_{3}.

As observed in Definition
1.20
,

\varphi_{+}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\omega}(q_{1})\bigr)\subset\mathfrak{go}(q_{2})
and

\varphi_{-}\bigl(\boldsymbol{\omega}(q_{1})\bigr)\subset\mathfrak{go}(q_{3})
, hence

\varphi_{+}(x_{1}y_{1})\in\mathfrak{go}(q_{2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varphi_{-}(x_{1}y_{1})\in\mathfrak{go}(q_{3})\qquad\text{for all $x_{1}$, $y_{1}\in V_{1}$.}

The definition of
\varphi
yields

\varphi_{+}(x_{1}y_{1})=r_{x_{1}}\ell_{y_{1}}
and
\varphi_{-}(x_{1}y_{1})=\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}
, hence by (
3.3
)

\displaystyle\dot{\mu}\bigl(\varphi_{+}(x_{1}y_{1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{+}(x_{1}y_{1})+\varphi_{+}(y_{1}x_{1})=b_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})

and

\displaystyle\dot{\mu}\bigl(\varphi_{-}(x_{1}y_{1})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\varphi_{-}(x_{1}y_{1})+\varphi_{-}(y_{1}x_{1})=b_{1}(x_{1},y_{1}).

Since
r_{x_{1}}\ell_{y_{1}}\in\mathfrak{go}(q_{2})
and

\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}\in\mathfrak{go}(q_{3})
, it follows from
Proposition
1.11

that for
x_{1}
,

y_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
and
x_{3}\in V_{3}

(3.4)

b_{2}(r_{x_{1}}\ell_{y_{1}}(x_{2}),x_{2})=b_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})q_{2}(x_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad b_{3}(\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}(x_{3}),x_{3})=b_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})q_{3}(x_{3}).

If
x_{1}\in V_{1}
is nonzero, there exists
y_{1}\in V_{1}
such that

b_{1}(x_{1},y_{1})=1
. From (
3.2
) and (
3.3
)
we derive for all
x_{2}\in V_{2}

b_{3}\bigl(\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2}),\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\bigr)=b_{2}\bigl(r_{x_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2}),r_{y_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\bigr)=q_{1}(x_{1})b_{2}\bigl(x_{2},r_{y_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\bigr).

But (
3.4
) yields

b_{3}\bigl(\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2}),\ell_{x_{1}}r_{y_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\bigr)=q_{3}\bigl(\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\bigr)\quad\text{and}\quad b_{2}\bigl(x_{2},r_{y_{1}}\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\bigr)=q_{2}(x_{2}),

hence

q_{3}\bigl(\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\bigr)=q_{1}(x_{1})q_{2}(x_{2})\qquad\text{for all $x_{1}\in V_{1}$, $x_{2}\in V_{2}$ with $x_{1}\neq 0$.}

This equation obviously also holds for
x_{1}=0
. Therefore, defining

*_{3}\colon V_{1}\times V_{2}\to V_{3}\qquad\text{by}\quad x_{1}*_{3}x_{2}=\ell_{x_{1}}(x_{2})\qquad\text{for $x_{1}\in V_{1}$ and $x_{2}\in V_{2}$,}

we see that
*_{3}
is a composition of
(V_{1},q_{1})
,
(V_{2},q_{2})
and

(V_{3},q_{3})
. Let also

x_{3}*_{2}x_{1}=r_{x_{1}}(x_{3})\qquad\text{for $x_{3}\in V_{3}$ and $x_{1}\in V_{1}$.}

From (
3.2
) it follows that
b_{2}(x_{3}*_{2}x_{1},x_{2})=b_{3}(x_{3},x_{1}*_{3}x_{2})
for
x_{1}\in V_{1}
,
x_{2}\in V_{2}
and
x_{3}\in V_{3}
, hence Proposition
2.1
shows that
*_{2}
is the
derived composition of
(V_{3},q_{3})
,

(V_{1},q_{1})
and
(V_{2},q_{2})
. Therefore,
\varphi_{0}=C_{0}(\alpha)
for

\alpha\colon V_{1}\to\End(V_{2}\oplus V_{3})
mapping
x_{1}\in V_{1}
to

\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}0&r_{x_{1}}\\
\ell_{x_{1}}&0\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)
. We thus see that
\mathcal{T}=\End(\mathcal{C})

for
\mathcal{C}=\bigl((V_{1},q_{1}),\,(V_{2},q_{2}),\,(V_{3},q_{3}),\,*_{3}\bigr)
.
Proposition
3.1
shows that the composition
\mathcal{C}
is
uniquely determined up to similitude.
∎

3.2.
Similitudes of trialitarian triples

Throughout this subsection, we fix a trialitarian triple

\mathcal{T}=(\mathfrak{A}_{1},\,\mathfrak{A}_{2},\,\mathfrak{A}_{3},\,\varphi_{0})

with
\mathfrak{A}_{i}=(A_{1},\sigma_{i},\strf_{i})
a central simple algebra with
quadratic pair of degree
8
over an arbitrary field
F
for
i=1
,

2
,
3
.
The algebraic group scheme
\BAut(\mathcal{T})
of
automorphisms of
\mathcal{T}
is defined as
follows: for any
commutative
F
-algebra
R
, the group
\BAut(\mathcal{T})(R)
consists of the
triples
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\gamma_{3})\in\BAut(\mathfrak{A}_{1})(R)\times\BAut(\mathfrak{A}_{2})(R)\times\BAut(\mathfrak{A}_{3})(R)
that
make the following square commute:

(3.5)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.57936pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.09326pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.09326pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 44.09326pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern
